## Chapter 20: The Arsenal

**chapter_id**: `chapter-20-the-arsenal`
**word_count**: 1563
**sections**: 7

- 20.1 Blue Scorpion Peptide
- 20.2 A Scorpion, a Snail, and a Lizard
- 20.3 Why the Gate Exists
- 20.4 BPC-157
- 20.5 The Ozempic Paradox
- 20.6 Three Floors. Pick Yours.
- 20.11 Moving On Up

### Body

The place where nature hands us an idea and science decides whether it becomes medicine or a scam.

The science is more complicated.

20.1 Blue Scorpion Peptide

The product is called Escozine. The proposed mechanism is biologically plausible. The clinical evidence is not. The biggest risk is that you spend $800 on a small bottle of saltwater with a Caribbean accent. And it does not work.

To be fair, scorpion venom is not the worst pitch in the wellness aisle. The molecule mirrors a class of FDA-approved therapies worth billions. They are called peptides. Short chains of amino acids that command biological signaling. Generally, two to fifty amino acids long. Short enough to understand. Powerful enough to run your body. Spiders, sea anemones, and pit vipers weaponized peptides hundreds of millions of years ago. Humans only recently started taking notes.

20.2 A Scorpion, a Snail, and a Lizard

A scorpion, a snail, and a lizard walk into a pharmacy.

The juice from the scorpion gets sold at a gas station next to CBD gummies. The snail toxin is featured in a surgically implanted pump that costs six figures. The lizard's saliva builds a pharmaceutical empire worth more than most countries.

All three are peptides.

The cone snail. Conus magus paralyzes fish with peptide-loaded harpoons. One of those peptides became Prialt, FDA-approved in 2004 to block N-type calcium channels and shut down pain signals at the spinal cord without the addiction risk of opioids. Prialt requires an intrathecal pump with firmware updates. That is a pain in the neck. But for some refractory pain patients, it is the only off-ramp from morphine or fentanyl.

The Gila monster. Exendin-4 was discovered in the saliva of a venomous lizard native to the American Southwest. It mimics GLP-1, the gut hormone that regulates blood sugar, satiety, and appetite. The lizard version is far more stable in the bloodstream than the human version, which degrades in minutes.

From that single molecule came exenatide, then liraglutide, then semaglutide (Ozempic), then tirzepatide (Mounjaro), and now retatrutide. Each generation more potent. And more profitable. GLP-1 drugs already generate tens of billions of dollars per year. The Gila monster has not received a royalty check.

Nature has cheat codes. Pharmaceutical companies have patent lawyers. The molecule does not care who owns it. But your access to it does.

20.3 Why the Gate Exists

Roughly half of all approved pharmaceuticals began as something humans were already chewing, brewing, or smearing on wounds centuries ago. Plants, fungi, venoms, and microbes that worked long before patents existed.

When a compound is cheap and freely occurring, there is little incentive for a patent. You cannot patent nature. No patent means no monopoly. No nine-figure trial. No FDA stamp.

That is why some therapies die on the vine. Other times, they get pushed out of the way.

One example is N-acetylcysteine. In July 2020, the FDA wanted it off supplement shelves. NAC is a $0.03 amino acid derivative that hospitals have used for decades as the antidote for Tylenol overdose. Walgreens sells it next to vitamin C. The FDA's argument was that NAC had originally been approved as a pharmaceutical, so it could no longer be a supplement. Manufacturers got warning letters. Amazon pulled it.

Industry pushback eventually forced the FDA's retreat in 2022. The bigger lesson landed.

If it is cheap, it can be ignored. If it works, it can be in the way. If it threatens profit, it can be regulated into oblivion.

20.4 BPC-157

Where NAC was nearly pushed out, BPC-157 was never let in.

BPC stands for Body Protection Compound. The 157 marks a fifteen-amino-acid sequence.

Gly-Glu-Pro-Pro-Pro-Gly-Lys-Pro-Ala-Asp-Asp-Ala-Gly-Leu-Val

BPC-157 is a synthetic fragment of a protein your stomach already makes. In animal models it accelerates healing of tendons, ligaments, nerves, gut lining, and connective tissue at a pace that borders on implausible. Croatian researcher Predrag Sikiric and his group have published over 200 papers on it.

The World Anti-Doping Agency (WADA) banned BPC-157 in 2022. Healing too fast is considered an unfair advantage. The FDA then slapped it into Category 2 in 2023, effectively kicking it out to the streets.

This created a ridiculous regulatory puzzle:

Doctors have wide latitude to prescribe experimental compounds when they think it’s right for the patient. That’s the practice of medicine. The FDA doesn’t directly control what doctors write on a pad. What they do control is what pharmacies are allowed to put in the bottle. If the substance isn’t on the official 503A Bulks List, pharmacies risk getting hammered for filling it.

Plot twist. In 2026, BPC-157 was removed from Category 2. Sounds like a green light? Not quite. Getting kicked off the “no” list doesn’t automatically put it on the “yes” list. It’s now in bureaucratic limbo, scheduled for formal review by the Pharmacy Compounding Advisory Committee. Until then, it remains what it’s always been: not quite legal, not quite illegal. Same story for about a dozen other peptides.

Even if BPC-157 gets cleared for compounding, it still won’t be FDA approved. Not until a billionaire funds the trials out of the goodness of their heart. Don’t hold your breath.

20.5 The Ozempic Paradox

BPC-157 is what happens when a peptide cannot be owned. Ozempic is what happens when one can.

Semaglutide is the synthetic analog of GLP-1. You cannot patent a molecule that already exists in your small intestine. But if there is enough money to be made, you can patent the workaround. Synthetic copies. Amino acid substitutions. Half-life extensions. Pre-filled auto-injectors. Proprietary dose cartridges.

The original semaglutide patent should have expired in March 2026. Through Patent Term Adjustments, Novo Nordisk pushed U.S. exclusivity to December 2031. Conservative estimates project an additional $166 billion in revenue from those five extra years. That is enough money to buy Iceland twice and have change left over for a yacht.

During shortages in 2024, compounding pharmacies were temporarily allowed to produce off-brand versions. The compounded versions were better medicine. Lower cost. Custom dosing. Less nausea. Less muscle wasting. Broader access. When the shortage was declared "over," the compounded options were stripped away. Not because the molecule became unsafe. Because the competition became unbearable.

In January 2026, Strive Compounding Pharmacy filed a federal antitrust lawsuit against Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk, alleging coordinated suppression of competition. Strive is taking on the Rockefeller and Carnegie of modern medicine. The same lineage of fortunes that built the Flexner Report in 1910, redrew the American medical school map, and decided which kinds of healing got funded for the next century.

Bold strategy.

The argument is obvious. This was never about safety. It is about market control.

So where does The Cockroach Diet stand on peptides?

We love them.

But they are not toys. They are Formula 1 cars and fighter jets. Powerful in trained hands. But you better know exactly what you are doing.

Under proper clinical guidance, certain peptides are real game-changers. FDA-approved options include Tesamorelin, PT-141, and the GLP-1 class. Others still live in the gray market. ARA-290. CJC-1295. Ipamorelin. BPC-157. TB-500. Thymosin α-1. The list keeps shifting.

As laid out in Chapter 6, peptides belong in the light. Backed by rigorous research, pristine manufacturing, and real medical oversight.

We are allowed to guzzle endless alcohol, eat plastic-laden garbage, and marinate in forever chemicals. Yet we are blocked from using precise molecular tools to repair and upgrade our own biology. That is not caution. It is insanity.

The regulations must change.

Peptides are only one floor of the arsenal. Three floors total. There are plenty of other powerful therapies at your disposal.

20.6 Three Floors. Pick Yours.

Every molecule in the arsenal you are about to open has a story. Some were borrowed from your own gastric juice. Some were weaponized by corporations and sold back to you at a thousand percent markup. Some were buried by regulators who decided exile was easier than understanding.

The chapters ahead contain the most powerful tools in regenerative medicine. None of them replace the STARTED protocol. None of them work without the foundation you built in the 12-Week Rollout. They are amplifiers, not substitutes.

Sequence still matters. With these interventions we will take the same approach. Always start small. Low risk. High reward.

The arsenal has three floors.

The first floor is cheap and accessible.

The second floor is more power and precision.

The third floor is the heavy equipment.

We cannot possibly list all the tools in the toolbox in a single book. But we don’t need to. Appendix A and lists a hundred heros. And provides AI prompts that can accelerate education. It looks like this.

MEDICAL DISCLAIMER: For educational purposes only. All interventions carry risks and should be administered under qualified medical supervision. Inclusion does not imply endorsement. Dosing reflects best available data at publication.

The arsenal keeps expanding. But the fundamentals always work.

20.11 Moving On Up

Level I is complete.

Most self-help books end here. Get your labs. Eat clean. Lift heavy. Take your supplements. Live to ninety. The end.

Most self-help books are wrong.

A body that lives to ninety with no purpose is just a longer wait in line. The cockroach survives. The cockroach also thrives. There is a difference.

You built the vehicle.

Time to figure out where to drive it.

Level-up.

— End of Chapter 20 —

Level 2. When survival is not enough

---

## Chapter 21: Cockroach in the ICU

**chapter_id**: `chapter-21-cockroach-in-the-icu`
**word_count**: 4194
**sections**: 12

- 21.1 Teahupoʻo
- 21.2 The First ER
- 21.3 What Happened
- 21.4 Confirmation or Confabulation
- 21.5 The Timeline
- 21.6 The Hospital
- 21.7 Another Mistake
- 21.8 The Magic Move
- 21.9 The Meltdown
- 21.10 CliffsNotes on Recovery
- 21.11 Where I Am Now
- 21.12 The Final Lesson

### Body

The cockroach that refused to die.

I do not go to the hospital often. But when I do, I go big.

Thirty years ago, it was a flesh-eating staph infection. This time it was progressive paralysis with an atypical presentation. In between, I barely got sick. Never had a surgery. Even kept my wisdom teeth. When life hit me, it skipped the warm-up rounds.

What follows is what happens when a medical professional becomes the patient.

21.1 Teahupoʻo

The paralysis hit me in Tahiti.

But the illness started long before that. The myocarditis, the brain fog, the crazy labs. All smoldering. They ignited while I was paddling into overhead surf at one of the heaviest waves on the planet. Teahupoʻo. Hard to pronounce, hard to ride. Not the ideal spot to develop double vision, slurred speech, and deadweight arms.

Some local legends were trading off on 4-6 foot barrels at the main peak. I sat down the line at the West Bowl waiting for a wide one. My vision started to blur. I felt dizzy. Sudden difficulty swallowing. I thumped my head a couple times hoping for a reset. After thirty minutes, I knew something was wrong. I needed to get back to land.

Jamie, my friend from Hawaii, was waiting on the inside on the jet ski. Our ride home. The fastest way to get to him was to catch one in. Like clockwork, a set wave appeared on the horizon. On a different day, this would have been the wave that made the trip.

On this day, I paddled in, tried to stand up, and went over the falls. Rode the whole wave underwater, bouncing off the reef. When I surfaced it was straight into a nightmare. I could not swim, could not breathe properly, and my arms hung like wet rope.

The Cockroach Diet requires calculated risks, not reckless ones. There is always a safety plan. Jamie was not only our strike team leader but a trained firefighter and water-rescue professional. He saw me struggling and whipped the jet ski around for a pickup just outside the impact zone.

"What's up?"

"I don't know. I can't swim, can't see."

"How long were you under?"

"Not long. It started before the wipeout."

"What?"

"Maybe food poisoning. Botulism. Or a stroke."

We laughed. The kind of laugh used when the alternative is panic. Stroke was a real possibility. Vertebral artery dissection happens in athletes with a history of spinal trauma. My symptoms checked all the boxes. Dizziness, ataxia, double vision, vertebral fracture. That was the worst-case scenario. Best-case was a bad hit of reef fish. Food poisoning. Or botulism sneaking into a recent reef-rash wound.

By the time we made it back to the dock I had a differential diagnosis in mind. With the wireless in range, I entered my search phrase. What is like Guillain-Barré but starts with double vision, dysphagia, and cervical weakness?

Miller Fisher Syndrome topped the list. I’d never heard of it. But good prompts get good answers. I flew home the next day for confirmation.

21.2 The First ER

The first Emergency Department discharged me without a blood test. To be fair, my speech was so slurred they probably thought I was drunk when I requested “labs for anti-GQ1b ganglioside antibodies to confirm Guillain-Barré variant known as Miller-Fisher syndrome.”

Instead of arguing, I left. I was still in denial myself. Maybe it was some exotic disease. Dengue fever. West Nile virus. Zika.

In the back of my mind, I knew it wasn't. I just wasn't ready to accept the truth.

Over the next two days, my legs started to fail. I could not lift my head. Speech became unintelligible. Swallowing was almost impossible. Had to use a handheld suction device to clear saliva. The things we take for granted.

I wrote the presumed diagnosis on a piece of paper and went to a different hospital.

The neurologist was extraordinary. She ran every test I requested and several I did not think to ask for. Myasthenia gravis. Herpes. Even syphilis. All negative. No smoking gun. I was about to discharge when the spinal tap showed elevated protein in the cerebrospinal fluid.

That was enough. They rushed me to the ICU and prepped the respiratory team in case my airway collapsed.

I had won a one-in-a-million lottery ticket. The prize sucked.

Even with the right diagnosis in hand, the system can send you home. Not because the doctors are bad. Because the decision tree is built for catastrophe, not uncertainty. That was the first lesson. Don't waste time. Find a provider who cares and doesn't discount your story. Shout out to Dr. Emily Sofroni for being that human.

21.3 What Happened

Most of the specialists had never heard of Miller Fisher Syndrome. Guillain-Barré was close enough for the working diagnosis.

Here is the ugly little mechanism.

A pathogen hits the body. A virus, a vaccine, a parasite. Some foreign invader walks through the gate, and the immune system does what it was built to do. It sounds the alarm. It mobilizes the troops. It manufactures antibodies with one job. Find the enemy and destroy it.

Then the tragedy begins.

The enemy looks too much like you.

Through molecular mimicry, antibodies built to kill the intruder begin mistaking pieces of your own nervous system for the threat. Friend and foe blur into the same silhouette. The body raises its sword and chops itself down.

It strips the insulation off your nerves. It chews through the cables that carry electrical signals. Muscles weaken. Reflexes vanish. Eyes stop obeying. Balance disappears. Breathing. Swallowing. Walking. Every simple function you never thanked God for, gone.

The variants have horror-movie acronyms. Mine started as Miller Fisher, drifted into a pharyngeal-cervical-brachial overlap, then morphed into a GBS/AMAN hybrid. Whatever you want to call it, within days I went from paddling Teahupoʻo to being unable to open a juice box.

Everyone asks how it happened.

The honest answer is nobody knows for certain. Correlation is not causation. A timeline is not proof.

But I had a theory.

21.4 Confirmation or Confabulation

I blamed the COVID vaccine. Specifically, the third jab.

That sentence angers everyone. Half think I'm a sheep for taking any. The other half think I'm a heretic for questioning Big Pharma. Anger is not an argument.

The lazy mind picks a side. Safe and effective. Or poison. Reality is nastier. A medical intervention can help one person and harm another. A technology can be brilliant and misapplied. A population-level benefit can hide an individual-level disaster. A rare complication is still rare until it happens to you.

Then it becomes one hundred percent of your life.

At UCLA West Hills, I shared my theory with a molecular biologist now working as a hospitalist. He agreed. Then put an exclamation point on it.

The spike protein was bad enough whether you had a vaccine or not. The platform was another problem.

In 2023, Nature published a paper showing that the modified RNA base (N-methylpseudouridine) used in several mRNA platforms can cause the ribosome to slip while reading the message. The ribosome is the tiny machine that reads genetic instructions and builds proteins. If the ribosome reads the recipe correctly, it makes the intended spike protein. If it slips by one letter (+1 frameshifting), the entire recipe changes from that point onward. The cell then produces unintended peptide fragments. Scrambled side products that were never part of the advertised antigen.

Science covered the finding under a headline that, depending on how you read it, is either reassuring or terrifying.

"mRNA vaccines may make unintended proteins, but there's no evidence of harm."

No one wants unintended proteins in their body. The same way they do not want to experiment with untested peptides shipped from a hyperlink on a Reddit thread. The body recognizes these chains of amino acids the way you recognize your family's faces. Uninvited strangers wandering through the house can cause a violent reaction.

Therapeutic peptides come with a Certificate of Analysis. Purity. Structure. Batch tested for LPS, TFA, endotoxins, heavy metals. Every result documented. The legality is heavily scrutinized. We just spent a whole chapter discussing the challenge prescribing peptides for health and longevity.

But with the vaccine mandate, millions of people injected themselves with junk peptides. That is scientific fact neither side debates. The irony writes itself.

What is open for debate is whether it matters.

Pro-vaccine scientists with industry ties were quoted saying that frameshifting could be helpful. Like hitting an old engine that won't turn over with a wrench. The shock frees a stuck starter, the motor fires, crisis averted.

Anti-vaccine scientists questioned their sanity. Take the same wrench to a finely tuned motor and you knock parts out of alignment, bend a shaft, crack a head gasket.

Both realities can exist. That is medicine.

The labels eventually caught up with theory. Myocarditis and pericarditis are now printed on mRNA COVID vaccine inserts. GBS is listed for certain viral-vector COVID vaccines. Encephalitis and chronic autoimmune conditions show up in case reports, VAERS, and medical literature. The list is still growing.

NAD+ depletion via CD38 hyperactivation following repeated spike antigen exposure is mechanistically established and measurable. What remains unresolved: whether residual plasmid DNA contamination in some lots poses integration risk, whether the SV40 nuclear localization sequence meaningfully increases that risk, and whether repeated booster exposure drives T-cell exhaustion at clinically significant levels. The data is incomplete. The questions are not crazy.

The conclusion is informed consent matters.

Informed consent was not given. Only a mandate.

That was the crime.

Millions of people did not get a risk-benefit conversation. They got a deadline. Comply or lose your job. Comply or lose your school. Comply or lose your access to your dying mother. Comply or get called a conspiracy theorist at Thanksgiving for asking the question a doctor should have welcomed. The questions were not anti-science. They were the first step of science.

Trust the science was a slogan made up by a marketing team. That’s not how it works. So now deal with the consequences. A whole generation learned that the system would punish them for thinking. That lesson did more damage than any peptide.

21.5 The Timeline

My own timeline was clear enough. Right after the booster, myocarditis showed up. Over the next six months my labs went from pristine to obscene. I was not worried. Until the brain fog moved in. Then the Anti-CASPR2 antibodies showed up. Those were concerning.

Then I got paralyzed. Then I was the ICU patient.

Even a Level III cockroach can screw up. I went against my gut and complied with a mandate I did not agree with. Jacked up a perfectly good immune system for a virus I was already immune to. Injected an Emergency Use Authorization technology that had unknown long-term consequences.

I wrote up my case study and submitted it for publication. Denied.

Not surprising. Adults are like overgrown children. The cliques still matter. The dogma is religion. It will take years, but the data will eventually surface.

The deeper lesson is not the vaccine. The deeper lesson is the delay.

When the first sparks appear, reactive medicine waits. For smoke. Then flame. Then collapse. It sprays water on a building already on fire and calls it a heroic act. Proactive medicine moves earlier. It extinguishes the ember before the news crew shows up. Not as exciting of a story. Not as well funded either.

I saw the firestorm coming. I did not ignore it. But I didn’t do enough to repair my biology while I still had the chance.

Millions of people are doing the same thing right now with their own chronic illness. They see the smoke. They smell the wire melting. They know something is wrong.

And they wait for a crisis to become an emergency. Just like I did.

21.6 The Hospital

The ICU room was nice.

Other aspects were not.

I was tethered to an EKG, an IV, a pulse oximeter, and a blood pressure cuff that inflated every fifteen minutes. The hospital is a terrible place to get sunlight or sleep. Goodbye, STARTED protocol.

I canceled the midnight-to-seven vitals. The other tests never stopped.

The nerve-conduction study showed wide axonal damage. Translation: my nerves were toast. The prognosis was 18 months to recovery with no guarantee of full restoration. I knew a younger patient with a similar condition still in a wheelchair a decade later.

Day five in the ICU, friends from grade school showed up. Under the circumstances, it was a splendid reunion. My best friend Matt stopped by. Every time I tried to speak, the words came out so slurred I reverted to smiles and nods.

My pincer grasp was gone. Mom had to peel back the foil on a cranberry juice for me. Then I choked so violently on a baby-sized sip that the specialists prepped for intubation.

I waved them off.

I could handle being oxygen-starved for a few minutes during a choking spell. What I could not control were nosocomial infections. Ventilator-associated pneumonia is too common, too nasty, and too real.

That is how Pops went out.

Ninety-nine years old. Tougher than leather. His doctor recommended laparoscopic cholecystectomy. Routine gallbladder surgery. The surgery went beautifully. The hospital-acquired pneumonia killed him.

The nurse brought me a thickened substitute that was supposed to be food. The texture was so disgusting my twelve-year-old nephew would not try it for a ten dollar dare.

Matt reminded him about the time I ate a cockroach for a dollar.

At least we could still laugh.

21.7 Another Mistake

There were two mainstream treatments for my illness. Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) floods the system with donated antibodies and can blunt the autoimmune attack. Plasmapheresis removes the harmful antibodies entirely and throws them in the trash.

Plasmapheresis was my preference. It was not available at that hospital.

The IVIG helped a little. Speech improved. Double vision, swallowing, ataxia, and limb weakness persisted. When the hospital ran out of moves, they discharged me.

Still not recovering a month later, I decided to fly to Hawaii. Oxygen-rich air, sunlight, ocean, sauna, cold plunge, and a beautiful place to rehab. Jamie, who had pulled me from the surf in Tahiti, had a place on the North Shore ready to go.

It was too early. I declined the airport wheelchair but shouldn’t have. My slow steps from the gate to curbside felt like the Death Zone on Everest. I was dead on arrival.

Following advice from a respected integrative physician, I tried Thymosin Alpha-1, a naturally occurring 28-amino-acid peptide from the thymus gland. On paper the reasoning was sound. Tα1 has literature supporting immune regulation, T-cell modulation, and effects on inflammatory balance. The goal was to recalibrate a dysregulated immune system. Thymosin Alpha-1 is approved in 35 countries. The clinical data is robust.

Still, it felt counterintuitive. The last thing I wanted was more firepower fueling the attack. But I read the research paper. I considered it carefully. I gave it a shot.

I crashed inside an hour.

Severe ataxia. Right arm paralysis. Difficulty breathing for 24 hours. Turned a beautiful beach in Hawaii into a nightmare. The right arm took months to return to baseline. Two fingers on my right hand are still not back.

Lesson burned in. Peptides are powerful in both directions. The wrong dose at the wrong time in the wrong person can be devastating. No matter how promising the research.

I cut the Hawaii trip short and readmitted myself to the hospital.

21.8 The Magic Move

The silver lining of the Hawaii disaster was leverage. It motivated me not to settle. I needed five sessions of therapeutic plasmapheresis. Now I demanded it.

Pull the blood out. Separate the plasma carrying autoantibodies, immune complexes, inflammatory debris, and whatever else was floating through the swamp. Throw the bad plasma in the trash. Replace it with clean albumin. Pump the refreshed blood back in.

In short, clean the pipes. Simple. Beautiful. The three-hour sessions with a 13 gauge sticking out of my neck was not fun. But the hassle was worth it.

The first session produced a dramatic response. I could rise onto the ball of one foot. Single-leg plantar flexion. My one-legged tippy toe was a small miracle. Then I picked up a viral URI during hospitalization that blunted sessions three through five. Immune system ramped up again. Progress stalled. Nothing comes easy on the road to recovery.

Something else happened during plasmapheresis I did not expect.

My brain came back.

The cognitive improvement was massive. What I thought was 100 percent was not even close. I had been operating with a chemical backpack I could not see.

Plasmapheresis did not just interrupt the autoimmune cycle. It felt like it cleared years of accumulated junk. Occupational exposures from firefighting, travel, smoke, metals, solvents, and the invisible residue of a very adventurous life. Maybe even some microplastics.

Anecdotal? Absolutely. But it was the closest thing to an oil change I’ve ever felt.

Reactive medicine reserves the procedure for certain catastrophes. Proactive medicine is studying it as a longevity intervention. Especially for people carrying decades of bioaccumulated inflammatory burden.

Forget EBOO, ozone, Weber laser. Don't settle. Plasmapheresis is the real deal.

21.9 The Meltdown

Two months later. The pace of nerve regeneration was frustrating.

Slower than molasses in January as Pops would say. Like him, I felt 100 years old. Going to the bathroom was my WOD. Had to pull myself up the stairs with the railing. Muscle mass was gone. My 12-year-old nephew could have whipped me in an MMA match.

Humbling does not cover it.

Imagine waking up with your mind on fire, ready to attack the day, then remembering the moment your feet hit the floor that your legs are made of concrete.

One morning I broke. I stared in the mirror at a man who looked fine but was locked inside a body that refused to cooperate. Logically, I knew people were suffering more than me. I had no right to complain. That only made it worse.

So, I had a prison riot.

I threw glass salt-and-pepper shakers against the tile floor. They exploded. One more mess I did not have the strength to clean up.

Then I unleashed a primal scream and shook my fist at the heavens. It felt pretty good. A full guttural roar that alarmed the neighbors. Call it sympathetic discharge, vagal release, or exorcism of the nervous system. Sometimes the body only listens when you stop asking politely.

Then I hit the kill switch. Fasted for 36 hours to see if I'd die. Dragged myself into a 180-degree sauna for 40 minutes until I was close to blacking out. Not suicidal. Pissed off. Crawled from the sauna to the cold pool and let the shock snap my brain back online.

July 21 was the pivot point. Not a miracle. A shift.

The attack was over. I could feel regeneration back in my bones. Progress stayed slow. But for the first time, it was moving forward instead of sideways.

It was time to count the casualties.

21.10 CliffsNotes on Recovery

The recovery therapies summarized below draw on peripheral nerve regeneration biology, mitochondrial recovery research, growth-hormone literature, and post-vaccine myocarditis surveillance data referenced in the chapter endnotes.

Peripheral nerves grow slow. Roughly 1 millimeter per day under ideal conditions. At 6'4", that made recovery a marathon measured in months, not days.

The goal was not recovery. Recovery returns you to the man who got sick. The Cockroach Code demands 110 percent. I aimed to be better than ever.

I cycled peptides, growth factors, senolytics, mitochondrial agents, hormones, oxygen therapies, and experimental compounds for months. Some helped. Some did nothing. Some I still cannot tell.

Six interventions earned starring roles.

NAD+. 100 mg IM every 24 to 48 hours. The metabolic foundation. In my darkest hour, it gave me enough energy to make it to the bathroom without a wheelchair.

Creatine monohydrate. 10 to 20 grams per day. Cheap, well-studied, criminally underrated. It helped halt the muscle loss and supported strength recovery when my nervous system was barely cooperating. Synergistic for neuroprotection.

Pharmaceutical HGH. 3 IU nightly for 30 days. The big gun. Growth hormone and IGF-1 signaling matter for tissue repair, remyelination, and axonal recovery. The reflexive fear that physiologic-dose HGH feeds cancer is more simplistic than the literature supports.

HBOT. 2.4 ATA, ninety minutes, forty sessions. At therapeutic pressure, dissolved oxygen in plasma rises dramatically and drives oxygen into injured tissues. Hyperbaric was not an add-on. It was the accelerator.

Enclomiphene. 12.5 mg daily, two weeks on, one week off. Kept total testosterone optimal without shutting down endogenous production. Androgen signaling matters for muscle, nerve repair, motivation, and recovery.

Plasmapheresis. The cognitive improvement alone was worth the three-hour sessions.

As Hippocrates said, "Desperate diseases must have desperate remedies."

Here’s the CliffsNotes.

MITOCHONDRIAL

REPAIR & RECOVERY

HORMONAL & GH AXIS

IMMUNE

MOOD

I used a Turbosonic vibration plate for 10 minutes a day a few times per week. Needed movement even when the body couldn’t spark it. I also cycled every supplement you could think of. My bathroom counter looked like a CVS aisle run by a Russian peptide chemist.

The basics drove most of the recovery. I trained every day even when it was pathetic.

Five-pound curls where fifty used to be. Zero-inch vertical for months. Bench press was an empty bar. Forty-five pounds of humiliation. But every rep was a signal to the nervous system. Wake up. Rebuild. Reconnect.

Sleep mattered. I quit alcohol entirely. After plasmapheresis scrubbed me clean, sober rest felt so good I could not justify going back.

Time mattered too.

Axons move at the pace axons move. You can optimize the terrain, improve blood flow, support mitochondria, and train like a ninja with a vendetta. You cannot bully biology past its speed limit.

On the flipside, there’s a limited window for healing. After injury, Schwann cells shift into repair mode. They clear debris, secrete growth factors, and build a living scaffold to guide the regenerating nerve. That repair state is temporary. If the axon does not arrive in time, the road begins to disappear. The body does not just stop healing. Eventually, it starts working against you.

This is not unique to nerves. It is the pattern of chronic disease. Joints that ache for years become arthritic. Inflammation that simmers becomes autoimmune. Insulin resistance becomes diabetes. A herniated disc becomes a fusion. Chronic infection becomes cancer.

The body sends signals first. Pain, fatigue, brain fog, poor sleep, a lab value drifting in the wrong direction. Every signal is a request for intervention. Ignore the request long enough and it becomes a final verdict.

The difference between the person who heals and the person who manages decline is rarely genetics. It is timing.

The early, aggressive, relentless patients are the ones who recover more often. Everyone else manages.

Patience without intervention is just waiting for the window to close.

21.11 Where I Am Now

Today, I’m ninety-five percent recovered. 15% to go.

The right hand is still paralyzed in places, but my guitar skills have improved. Plenty of time to practice. Grip strength is not what it was. My feet are still growing back. My toes ache in the most annoying way possible. Who complains about achy toes?

The fatigue still ambushes me without warning. But I am lucky.

Last month a patient rolled into the clinic in a wheelchair. Same disease I had. But worse. He was told to make peace with it.

Six months later he started walking.

We don’t have a cure. We have a system. The same operating system in this book. Three levels. STARTED. 12-Week Rollouts. The three P’s. The Arsenal.

Test everything. Discard what fails. Double down on what works. Never accept someone else's ceiling for your recovery.

When the system says this is all we can offer, keep searching.

21.12 The Final Lesson

I thought I was invincible. I was not. Nobody is.

The cockroach survives not because it is the strongest or the fastest. It survives because it never stops adapting. It takes the hit, sheds the exoskeleton, and rebuilds harder.

That’s what I did. Not gracefully. Not without a full meltdown that terrified the neighbors and destroyed a perfectly good saltshaker.

But I rebuilt.

If you are reading this from a hospital bed, or from a body that does not work the way it used to, or from a diagnosis someone told you was permanent, hear this.

Progress is slow. Recovery is expensive. Healing is infuriating. But the comeback is real. Millimeter by millimeter. Plate by plate. Nerve by nerve.

You are not your diagnosis. The system's limitations are not your limitations.

Proactive medicine is my current Noble Pursuit. Treat the patient, not the population. Use precision tools while the window is still open. The reactive system will always be there if the building is burning.

My team gets you out before the smoke alarm goes off.

The cockroach refused to quit.

So do we.

Go forth and be indestructible.

— End of Chapter 21 —

---

## Chapter 22: Get AMPed

**chapter_id**: `chapter-22-get-amped`
**word_count**: 2728
**sections**: 7

- 22.1 A Noble Pursuit
- 22.2 The Starfish
- 22.3 What Hippocrates Actually Said
- 22.4 Graveyards
- 22.5 Holistic Headstones
- 22.6 The Drift
- 22.7 The Ridgeline

### Body

“Build a man fire and he’ll be warm for an hour. Set a man on fire, and he’ll be warm for the rest of his life.” – Sir Terence David John Pratchett

At Level I, exercise is mostly about you.

Close your rings. Hit your steps. Lift the weight. Build the body. Prove you own your time, muscles, and decisions.

At Level II, you still close your rings, but the workout is no longer just selfish time in the gym. Strength becomes service. Movement becomes contribution. Fitness leaves the mirror and enters the world.

The gym is getting boring by now anyway. Time to expand your physical activities. Wash your friend’s car. Do the dishes for your mom. Help someone move. Carry groceries. Mow the lawn. Stack firewood. Walk with someone who needs company. Coach a kid. Push a stroller.

These are not interruptions to your fitness plan. They are the point.

Don’t ditch the gym completely. Just bring someone with you. Teach your nephew to deadlift. Show your friend how to squat. Spot the beginner. Make strength contagious.

Level I built the body. Level II fires up your mind.

Fabricated adversity made you harder to kill. The Noble Pursuit makes you harder to corrupt. Create more than you consume. Carry more weight for people who cannot carry it themselves. That is the mission.

Build a family, a business, a book, a clinic, a body of work, a better version of yourself that becomes useful to someone else. Consumption drains you into the machine. Creation pulls you out of it.

A better body without a better mission is just a more efficient animal.

The AMP Framework

A → Adventure Step outside your comfort zone. Seek challenge, growth, and expertise.

M → Momentum Take small daily steps toward a productive goal. Track progress. It stacks.

P → Purpose Create instead of consume. Build something worth defending.

22.1 A Noble Pursuit

Get AMPed, stay AMPed. I was an EMT, paramedic, flight medic, fire captain, PA-C, and MD. The titles changed. So did the terrain. Disaster zones. Firegrounds. Helicopters. Mountain rescues. Emergency care for the underserved. Different uniforms, same job: pull people out of something terrible and send them home better than I found them.

The MD was the final upgrade. It was never about the title. Before medical school, I already knew I did not want the traditional path. I did not spend four years and half a million dollars so I could memorize billing codes, prescribe two antibiotics to a patient whose vitamin D was never checked, or open a med spa with marble counters and a neon sign about vitality.

The plan was bigger. Get the credential. Do the research. Build the protocols. Bring preventive and regenerative therapies out of the gray market and into clean, compliant pathways.

Then paralysis made the decision permanent.

The new goal is taking data to Congress and legalizing peptides and stem cells at the federal level. The adventure is on. The momentum is building. The purpose fires the mind and keeps my legs moving.

I still see patients one day a week. When I prescribe, I do it as a PA. Patients care about being heard and getting better. Even better if it takes less medication to get them there. We are working with the ACGME to help create a residency pathway for this kind of proactive medicine because the specialty does not exist yet, and it should.

The MD is for the Institutional Review Board, the oversight, the data capture, and the pressure that needs to be applied upstream.

My old Noble Pursuit was creating order inside chaos. My new one is dragging regenerative therapies into the mainstream. Keep people out of the hospital. Keep them out of decline. Stop telling them that feeling like trash is normal. Use well-studied tools to solve root problems before the building is on fire. Use AI to assist with data capture. Increase statistical power. Prove clinical significance.

Your Noble Pursuit may be different. Find it. Build the family, the company, the clinic, the book, the product, the farm, the mission, the body of work. Use a self-help book if you need the spark. Napoleon Hill already covered the prerequisite: conceive, believe, achieve.

Wanting more is easy. Building something useful with it is the test.

22.2 The Starfish

Ever heard this story? A violent storm rolls through a seaside town. By morning the beach is covered with thousands of starfish, stranded on the sand, drying in the sun. A boy walks the shoreline, picking them up one by one and throwing them back into the sea.

A fisherman watches from the jetty. Finally, he calls out.

"Hey kid. Look around. There are thousands of them. You cannot save them all. You are not going to make a difference."

The boy picks up another starfish, throws it back into the water, and replies.

"Made a difference for that one."

It's kindergarten wisdom. But that's the strategy. The fisherman was measuring success by scale. The boy was measuring success by responsibility. Most people see a problem too large to solve and use it as permission to do nothing. The system is too broken. The food supply is too poisoned. The kids are too addicted. The patients are too far gone.

The Cockroach Diet is for the select few who want to crawl out of the manufactured matrix and help the next person escape. We won't pay influencers to hype the mission. There's no energy to waste on a virtue-signaling podcast. We are not looking for accolades and exaltations from the gatekeepers. Quite the opposite.

Pick up one starfish. The Noble Pursuit starts with bending the knee.

22.3 What Hippocrates Actually Said

Humility in medicine is as important as bedside manner. But contrary to popular belief, Hippocrates did not write "First, do no harm." The original directive was more demanding:

ἀσκεῖν περὶ τὰ νοσήματα δύο, ὠφελεῖν ἢ μὴ βλάπτειν

"As to diseases, make a habit of two things. To help. Or at least, to do no harm."

The order matters. Help first. If you cannot help, at least do not make it worse.

Modern medicine reversed the emphasis. Do no harm became the whole moral posture. Avoid risk. Avoid liability. Avoid being wrong. Avoid the intervention until the patient is sick enough for a diagnosis code, a guideline, and a reimbursement pathway.

Helping is harder. It demands judgment and risk. It requires knowing the trajectory before the crash and acting on the uncertainty of the present. Doing nothing can be wise. Doing nothing can also be cowardice dressed as status quo.

That is the fork. Mainstream medicine has one failure mode. Alternative medicine has another. The most sensible path is not blind trust in either side. It is the narrow ridgeline between them. How you navigate your path is what makes your pursuit noble. Or not.

22.4 Graveyards

Medicine has a long history of being wrong with confidence.

In 1847 Ignaz Semmelweis told doctors to wash their hands. They mocked him. He noticed that women were dying of childbed fever after being examined by physicians who had just come from autopsies. His solution was simple. Wash your hands with chlorinated lime. Maternal deaths plummeted in his ward. The establishment still rejected him. He died at forty-seven in an asylum from a septic wound. He was right. He was just early.

In the 1940s and 50s, lobotomies were celebrated as cutting-edge psychiatry. Tens of thousands were performed in the United States. Families were told it was compassionate care. Walter Freeman drove an icepick through the eye socket and swept it across the frontal lobe like a swizzle stick. The technique won a Nobel Prize. JFK's sister, Rosemary Kennedy, was lobotomized at twenty-three and spent the rest of her life institutionalized.

Electroconvulsive therapy went the opposite direction. After One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest in 1975, ECT became a cultural symbol of cruelty and the stigma stuck. Modern ECT under general anesthesia delivers up to 70 to 90% remission in treatment-resistant depression, often faster than any medication, and remains one of the few relatively safe options during pregnancy.

One tool was overused and destroyed lives. Another was stigmatized and people suffered. That is medicine. Not good team versus bad team. Tools, timing, indications, risk, benefit, judgment.

It’s a stressful career. Stomach ulcers were blamed on stress for decades. Patients were told to relax and avoid spicy foods. Then Barry Marshall, an Australian physician with the appropriate amount of scientific insanity, drank a beaker of Helicobacter pylori to prove bacteria could cause gastritis and ulcers. He was ridiculed. Then vindicated. Then awarded the Nobel Prize twenty-one years later.

Psychiatry may be in the middle of its own H. pylori moment. In a Stanford-associated study of veterans with traumatic brain injury, ibogaine treatment was associated with rapid improvements in PTSD, depression, anxiety, disability, and cognition.

Hormone therapy for women followed its own disaster arc. Once hailed as a miracle, then buried by the Women's Health Initiative in 2002, then avoided for decades while millions of women suffered through symptoms that were often treatable. We unpack the wreckage in Chapter 24.

Mainstream medicine has been wrong plenty of times. Thalidomide and birth defects. Vioxx and heart attacks. Opioids and overdoses. But who hasn’t?

The alternative side has its own graveyard.

22.5 Holistic Headstones

Laetrile, marketed as "vitamin B-17," became the rebel cancer cure of the 1970s. It was distilled from apricot pits and released hydrogen cyanide in the body, the same compound used in gas chambers. Steve McQueen flew to Mexico for it in 1980. The cyanide killed him faster than the mesothelioma would have. Thousands followed his lead and skipped proven treatment because "natural" sounded safer than oncology.

Harry Hoxsey built an empire on herbal tonics and billboards screaming, “Cancer is curable now!” When his own herbal formula failed to help him, he quietly underwent conventional surgery and standard medical treatment.

Bob Marley refused amputation for a melanoma on his big toe and pursued alternative clinics in Germany. The cancer killed him at thirty-six. Steve Jobs delayed surgery for a highly treatable pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor while he chased acupuncture, juice fasts, and supplements. By the time he chose conventional treatment, it was too late.

Celebrity cases get the headlines. Stranger things happen in the corners nobody is watching. Miracle Mineral Solution is industrial bleach repackaged as a cure for autism, cancer, HIV, malaria, and whatever else the salesman needed it to cure that week. Parents have given chlorine dioxide enemas to autistic children for over a decade and were told the shredded intestinal lining coming out was "rope worms." It was their child's gut wall.

Detox culture promotes coffee enemas as cleansing, IV hydrogen peroxide that causes gas embolisms, and corrosive pastes like Black Salve that burn through healthy flesh while missing tumors. Turpentine as a parasite cleanse. Urine as a healing beverage.

There is always a sprinkle of truth. Stem-cell populations have been isolated from urine in laboratories. Drinking it has never regenerated anything except internet folklore.

Then there is the corner store. Kratom, sold as a "natural" alternative to opioids, has been linked to liver failure, kidney failure, seizures, coma, and death. Phenibut, the chill-out nootropic, produces benzodiazepine-grade withdrawal that has put users in the ICU. Tianeptine is an alternative antidepressant also known as “gas station heroin.”

Two columns. Same lesson.

Ego kills in a white coat. Ignorance kills in a hemp poncho. The road less traveled is paved with common sense.

22.6 The Drift

Every Noble Pursuit begins clean. Then the drift begins.

Medicine is one arc to study. The Flexner Report of 1910 was commissioned by the Carnegie Foundation and followed by hundreds of millions from John D. Rockefeller's General Education Board. It standardized medical education and closed the schools that did not align with pharmaceutical-based, patent-friendly medicine. Drugs that emerged from the new system were often derived from petrochemicals, the same molecular feedstock used to make fuel, fertilizers, pesticides, and plastics. Standard Oil with a stethoscope.

It wasn't all bad. Vertical integration was a game well-played by the men who built America. Efficient reform amid rampant quackery was necessary. Better science was the goal. But money was the boss. When greed grew out of control, nobody redirected the system. That is not how you win.

Fast forward to 2004. Dinesh Thakur was a director at Ranbaxy Laboratories. He discovered his employer was systematically faking data on drugs being shipped to America, Africa, and the WHO. Malaria drugs. Antibiotics. Lifeline medications. Some completely fabricated. Others contaminated with unidentified particles in vials.

He brought it to the board. They reviewed the findings. They did nothing.

He resigned and filed a confidential complaint with the FDA in 2005. They did nothing either.

The investigation ran for eight years while the bad drugs kept shipping.

In 2013, Ranbaxy pleaded guilty to seven federal felony charges for fabricating drug safety and efficacy data. The largest generic drug fraud case in American history.

The fine was $500 million.

While one arm of the FDA was building the criminal case, another arm awarded Ranbaxy exclusive rights to manufacture generic Lipitor. That single contract generated $600 million in revenue in six months.

Ranbaxy came out about $100 million ahead.

No one went to prison. The company was acquired, rebranded, absorbed. The machine kept running.

Ranbaxy was not the exception. It was the one we know about because one man refused to stay silent, even with threats against his life.

The cases not in the headlines are the ones where nobody took a stand.

The top five American pharmaceutical companies have individually paid billions in fines for the same playbook. Off-label marketing of drugs for unapproved uses. Kickbacks to doctors. Hidden safety data on cardiovascular events, suicidality, and addiction. Inflated pricing on government contracts. Fraudulent claims to Medicare and Medicaid. The slap on the wrist is the cost of doing business.

Alzheimer's research has been distorted by decades of amyloid fixation, fragile biomarkers, expensive monoclonals, and, in some cases, serious misconduct allegations at the highest levels of the field.

Alternative medicine began as a rebellion against this machine. But money is the final boss here too. Different fonts. More turmeric.

The drift is not unique to medicine. A church becomes a brand. A charity becomes a bureaucracy. A health movement becomes a supplement funnel. The Noble Pursuit becomes corrupt.

Your Noble Pursuit is not immune. Neither is mine.

That is why AMP matters. Adventure keeps you exposed to reality. Momentum keeps you honest through metrics. Purpose keeps the mission from collapsing into comfort.

Tend your Noble Pursuit daily. The drift never sleeps.

22.7 The Ridgeline

Mainstream and alternative. Both sides have saved lives. Both sides have buried people. The chapter has been about choosing the path between them.

Now, a new player has entered the chat.

AI-orchestrated evidence platforms are about to do for medicine what GPS did for navigation. The slow, expensive gold standard, the double-blind randomized controlled trial (RCT), costs $20 to $50 million and takes three to seven years to answer one narrow question in one narrow population. Then someone like you reads the abstract a decade later and tries to apply it to a body the trial never studied.

That model is on borrowed time.

Within the next decade, the most reliable medical evidence for many common conditions will not come from a single shiny RCT in the New England Journal of Medicine. It will come from continuously updated platforms that synthesize randomized trials, real-world data, genomics, wearables, and patient-reported outcomes from millions of people simultaneously. The FDA and EMA are already running pilots. Start-ups are moving even faster. A question that took twenty years and a billion dollars to answer will get solved in a week. With confidence intervals.

The bullshit detector is finally getting superpowers.

The age of "trust the gurus" is ending. The age of verifiable, high-resolution, continuously refined evidence is just beginning. The patient who shows up to a fifteen-minute appointment with their own data, their own analysis, and their own questions is not a problem to be managed. They are the future of the room.

One your health is dialed you will have plenty of time for your Noble Pursuit.

We are not wrong. We are just early.

Incurable to Unbreakable

— End of Chapter 22 —

---

## Chapter 23: Men’s Wellness

**chapter_id**: `chapter-23-men-s-wellness`
**word_count**: 1971
**sections**: 9

- 23.1 The Quiet Epidemic
- 23.2 The Labs
- 23.3 The Optimization Ladder
- 23.4 GH Secretagogues for IGF-1 Optimization
- 23.5 Vanity. Hair, Skin, and the Rest.
- 23.6 Low-Dose Cialis for Everything
- 23.7 Men’s Optimization Stack
- 23.8 Prostate and Sexual Health
- 23.9 The Bottom Line

### Body

“The world breaks everyone, and afterward, many are strong at the broken places.” – Ernest Hemingway

Testosterone in American men has been declining at roughly 1% per year since the 1980s. A 30-year-old man today has, on average, significantly less testosterone than his father did at the same age.

This is environmental castration.

Endocrine disruptors in plastics, pesticides, and processed food. Chronic sleep deprivation. Sedentary lifestyles. Seed oils. Alcohol. Stress. Obesity. Each one suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. Stack them all together and you get a generation of men who are fatigued, depressed, overweight, sexually dysfunctional, and told this is normal.

It is not normal. It is preventable. Most of it is reversible.

23.1 The Quiet Epidemic

The medical system’s response has been predictable. Ignore it until the patient demands help, then prescribe injectable testosterone replacement. A lifelong commitment that shuts down natural production, shrinks the testes, destroys fertility, and requires weekly injections. For most men, especially those under 40, this is the wrong first move.

There is a ladder. And injectable TRT is the last rung, not the first. The same AI prompt power is available for both the Men's and Women's Wellness sections. Use it.

23.2 The Labs

The Cockroach Baseline Panel from Chapter 15 covers most of what a man needs. The Week 2 decision tree in Chapter 18 handles the universal findings. Here are the specifics.

Hormonal panel. Total testosterone, free testosterone, bioavailable testosterone, SHBG, sensitive estradiol assay, LH, FSH, prolactin, DHEA-S, pregnenolone. A man can have a "normal" total testosterone of 500 ng/dL while his SHBG is 80 and his free testosterone is in the gutter. That man is functionally hypogonadal despite a normal-looking number.

Thyroid panel. TSH, free T3, free T4, reverse T3. Hypothyroidism in men is underdiagnosed and directly suppresses testosterone production, libido, and energy.

Metabolic and growth markers. Fasting insulin, HbA1c, IGF-1, hs-CRP, ferritin, vitamin D, ApoB, Lp(a), CBC with hematocrit. IGF-1 below 150 ng/mL warrants investigation. Hematocrit baseline matters because any testosterone optimization can increase red blood cell production and blood pressure.

Advanced. DUTCH test for full androgen metabolism. 5-alpha reductase activity, testosterone-to-DHT conversion, cortisol rhythm. PSA baseline if over 40. Estrogen metabolism pathways (2-OH, 4-OH, 16-OH). DIM and cruciferous vegetables are not just for women.

23.3 The Optimization Ladder

Before you inject anything, climb the bottom rungs. Most men can add 200 to 400 ng/dL of total testosterone by fixing the basics. STARTED on repeat. Reps are how we get shredded. Reps are how we learn.

Rung 1. Lifestyle. Free.

Lift heavy weights. Compound movements three to four times a week. The single most powerful testosterone intervention that exists. Resistance training stimulates LH release, increases androgen receptor density, and improves insulin sensitivity. All of which support endogenous testosterone production.

Sleep 7 to 9 hours in a cold dark room. Testosterone is produced primarily during deep sleep. Men who sleep fewer than 5 hours per night have testosterone levels equivalent to men 10 to 15 years older. One week of restricted sleep drops testosterone by 10 to 15%.

Quit or drastically reduce alcohol. Alcohol is directly toxic to Leydig cells, the testicular cells that produce testosterone. It increases aromatase activity, converting more testosterone to estradiol. This is why men who inject testosterone, then sit on the couch and drink beers don’t increase muscle. They increase man-boobs.

Eliminate soy and reduce xenoestrogen exposure. BPA, phthalates, and other endocrine disruptors in plastics, receipts, and personal care products accumulate and mimic estrogen. Switch to glass containers. Filter your water. Read labels.

Eat enough protein and healthy fats. Cholesterol is the precursor to every sex hormone. Low-fat diets suppress testosterone. Target at least 1.0 g protein per pound of lean body mass. Bodybuilders do 2.0. Eggs, red meat, fatty fish, beef liver, olive oil. Zinc 30-50 mg daily, magnesium 400+ mg, vitamin D3 5,000 to 10,000 IU are foundational cofactors.

Manage body fat. Adipose tissue contains aromatase, the enzyme that converts testosterone to estrogen. The sweet spot for most men is 12 to 18% body fat. Below 10% can paradoxically suppress testosterone. Endurance athletes often have low testosterone. That is fine. They run faster than you.

Rung 2. Targeted Supplements.

Boron, 6 to 12 mg daily. Reduces SHBG within one week, freeing bound testosterone into its bioavailable form. Also reduces estradiol. One of the most underrated interventions in men’s health. A $10 supplement that can functionally increase free testosterone by 25 to 30%.

Tongkat Ali, 200 to 400 mg extract. Clinical trials show increases in total and free testosterone, reductions in cortisol, and improvements in mood and body composition. Mechanism likely involves StAR protein upregulation, enhancing cholesterol transport into mitochondria for steroidogenesis.

Fadogia Agrestis, 600 mg daily, cycled 5 days on 2 off. May increase LH signaling to Leydig cells. Limited human data but promising animal studies. Cycle it. Long-term continuous use raises theoretical concerns about testicular oxidative stress. Stack with Tongkat Ali for synergy.

DIM, 200 to 300 mg daily. Supports healthy estrogen metabolism by shifting the 4-OH pathway toward the safer 2-OH pathway. Essential if estradiol is elevated or DUTCH shows unfavorable estrogen metabolite ratios.

Rung 3. Enclomiphene. Prescription. Fertility-Preserving.

If lifestyle and supplements do not bring testosterone into the 600 to 900 ng/dL range, enclomiphene is the next step. For most men, it should be tried before TRT.

Enclomiphene is the purified active isomer of clomiphene citrate. It blocks estrogen receptors in the hypothalamus, releasing the brake on GnRH, which increases LH and FSH. The result. Your own testes produce more testosterone. Clinical trials show total testosterone increases of 200 to 400 ng/dL within 6 to 16 weeks while maintaining or improving sperm counts.

The pattern is obvious. Injectable testosterone cypionate tells your brain to stop making it. Enclomiphene tells your brain to make more.

Enclomiphene is not FDA-approved for men as of 2026 but is available through compounding pharmacies with a prescription. It is well-tolerated with fewer estrogenic side effects than clomiphene (which contains the problematic zuclomiphene isomer). Monitor hematocrit, estradiol, and LH/FSH at 6 to 8 weeks. If estradiol rises excessively, pump more weights drink less booze. Then increase DIM or add a small dose of aromatase inhibitor once a week.

Rung 4. TRT. Last Resort.

Injectable testosterone is appropriate for primary hypogonadism (testicular failure), men over 50 who have exhausted other options, or men who do not plan to have children and want maximum optimization.

Bro science loves TRT for putting on size and we do not judge. Typical protocol is testosterone cypionate 100 to 200 mg per week IM or subcutaneous, split into two doses for more stable levels. Bodybuilders go way bigger.

TRT requires concurrent monitoring of hematocrit, estradiol, PSA, and lipids. Consider hCG 500 IU twice weekly to maintain testicular volume and intratesticular testosterone. Bank sperm before starting if fertility is important.

TRT works. It is powerful. But it is a one-way door for most men. Once you start, your HPG axis may not fully recover. Donating blood due to rising hematocrit is often necessary. Hair starts to thin faster for many. Estrogen can increase to undesirable levels.

On the other hand, injectable testosterone is a gamechanger for many men. There wouldn’t be hundreds of cash-pay clinics sprouting up if it wasn’t lucrative and effective business.

Just understand the science before you commit.

23.4 GH Secretagogues for IGF-1 Optimization

CJC-1295 (no-DAC) plus Ipamorelin stimulate natural GH pulsatility. Improves sleep, body composition, recovery, bone density, and skin quality. If IGF-1 is below 150 ng/mL, this stack can be transformative. For men over 60, a 3-month cycle of pharmaceutical HGH (1 to 3 IU nightly) may be more appropriate. Legal with a prescription. As cited in my own case, the cancer-fear narrative is not supported by decades of physiologic-dose data.

23.5 Vanity. Hair, Skin, and the Rest.

Men want to be buff, tan, and hairless. Or at least strategically haired.

Oral finasteride and dutasteride should be avoided because of post-finasteride syndrome (PFS). Persistent sexual, neuropsychiatric, and physical symptoms that can outlast the drug by years. A topical approach targets the scalp while minimizing systemic exposure. Compounding labs combine these into one product often with biotin and hydrocortisone or other synergistic components.

The hair loss industry is a $4 billion machine built on insecurity and before-and-after photos with suspiciously different lighting. Nutrafol’s organic supplement is popular enough for them to buy billboards at the World Series.

Impatient hair farmers fly to Turkey. A procedure that costs $20,000 in Beverly Hills costs $3,000 in Istanbul, with a free hotel and airport shuttle. Some men come back with a perfect hairline. Some come back looking like a doll that was repaired by a child. Look at 12-month post-op results, not the 3-month marketing photos where the swelling makes everything look thicker. Ask for the surgeon’s name, not the clinic’s Instagram handle. Do your research.

23.6 Low-Dose Cialis for Everything

Tadalafil (or sildenafil) is not just for erectile dysfunction. It improves endothelial function, microcirculation, exercise performance, and blood flow to every organ, including the brain. May be synergistic with HBOT and pre-workout. FDA-approved for daily use in ED and benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH).

There are millions of patients with a ton of data on this. But official guidance is still, “Randomized controlled trials are still needed to confirm a causal protective effect.”

TCD opinion: One of the most versatile and underappreciated molecules in men’s health.

23.7 Men’s Optimization Stack

Nine compounds organized by system. Full protocols and dosing in Appendix A. You don’t run these all at once. These are just options to run under good clinical guidance. If it’s legal in your jurisdiction of course.

23.8 Prostate and Sexual Health

Testosterone optimization does not cause prostate cancer. The "testosterone feeds cancer" myth has been debunked by multiple large-scale studies, including the Endogenous Hormones and Prostate Cancer Collaborative Group analysis of 18 prospective studies showing no association between circulating testosterone and prostate cancer risk.

Monitoring still matters. PSA baseline before starting any protocol. Repeat at 3 months and annually. Digital rectal exam annually after 40. If PSA rises more than 1.0 ng/mL in 12 months, investigate regardless of absolute level.

For erectile function. Fix testosterone, sleep, and cardiovascular health first. Low-dose daily tadalafil is the next layer. PT-141 for desire-based dysfunction. PDE5 inhibitors for vascular. If refractory, consider shockwave therapy (Li-ESWT). Strong evidence for neovascularization and endothelial regeneration in the corpora cavernosa. Six to twelve sessions. No drugs required. P-shot (PRP injection) is another option for libido and ED. Also popular for immediate but short-lived girth enhancement.

23.9 The Bottom Line

Men's wellness is not complicated. It is systematically ignored. There's more we didn't cover. The gut-testosterone axis. Sleep apnea. All of it findable with the 12-Week Rollout, good AI prompts, and a helpful clinician. The ladder is simple.

Step 1. Fix sleep, training, nutrition, and body fat.

Don’t forget this is Level II. You’re not getting off the hook with a simple review of body mechanics.

Men deserve to be happy. Not distracted, sedated, or discounted. Not optimized into a better-looking version of quiet desperation. No peptide, supplement, or prescription can rescue a man who keeps poisoning the machine.

The body is the prerequisite.

The Noble Pursuit is the real fight.

A man can fix every lab and still feel empty if he has no worthy battle. Low testosterone is real. So is burnout, loneliness, and depression. A man was built to carry weight. Find something meaningful to carry.

A family. A craft. A business. A cause. A skill. A mission. A problem that refuses to leave you alone until you solve it. The best pursuit is one that scares you. More responsibility, not less. Ditch the safety net.

Fulfillment is capacity aimed at something that matters.

A man without a fight will invent one against himself.

— End of Chapter 23 —

---

## Chapter 24: Women’s Wellness

**chapter_id**: `chapter-24-women-s-wellness`
